Text:"Trial by Trace: Benchmarking AI Agent Harnesses for Reliability and Failure Attribution TraceLite AI1,2,3,4,5 1Institute of Automation, CAS, 2Zhongguancun Academy 3Institute of Information Engineering, CAS 4Tsinghua University, 5Beijing Institute of Technology Abstract LLM agents are increasingly used to solve real-world problems, and their effectiveness depends on both the underlying models and the harnesses that support their operation. However, most existing benchmarks focus on model capabilities and overall agent performance, with limited attention to the evaluation of agent harnesses. To address this gap, we introduce TraceTrial-Bench, which organizes harness reliability into seven components covering context, execution, governance, lifecycle, observability, tools, and verification. We construct 95 expert-level, mechanism-oriented tasks with executable or state-based ground truth to support semantically equivalent comparisons while preserving the native behavior of each harness. We design Trajectory Tracing (TT) module to capture agent execution traces and introduce two-layer evaluation framework that separately assesses agent performance and harness reliability. In addition, we design Multi-source Evidence Acquisition (MEA) module to provide evidence from the agent context, harness, and execution environment for LLM-based evaluation, and develop Failure Attribution (FA) module that uses explicit mechanism chains to support attribution at the run level. Across 4 harnesses, 9 models, and 95 tasks, our evaluation of 3,420 runs yields macro-averaged harness scores ranging from 59.8% to 72.0%, while 31.1% of runs achieve task success despite failing at least one harness criterion. TraceTrial-Bench provides an auditable and reproducible foundation for comparing agent harnesses, diagnosing their failures, and studying how harness design interacts with model behavior. # Correspondence: contact@tracelite.ai Repository: https://github.com/TraceLite-AI/TraceTrial-Bench ("

People have long dreamed of an assistant that is always there to guide them and remind what they forget, from new dishes to lost keys. Powered by multimodal large language models (MLLMs), egocentric AI assistants on wearable devices are bringing this dream within reach: they see what the user sees and give instructions for users physical work when necessary. However, testing such assistance is challenging and usually requires real people. The expensive and rare real interaction data leaves key question unanswered: Can todays MLLMs truly help people in physical world? To answer this question, we propose InteractiveWorld, the first verifiable environment for closed-loop interaction between human and AI assistant. Unlike existing robotcentric simulators, InteractiveWorld lets the assistant guide human agent who observes the scene, understands instructions, and acts independently. It thus realistically simulates how AI assistant collaborates with human: the assistant instructs, the human acts, and the environment executes actions and updates what they see next. Building on it, we introduce InteractiveBench, the first dynamic benchmark for egocentric AI assistants, with 120 multi-turn tasks across six categories. In each task, the assistant guides the human in real time, and every instruction shapes the outcome, which is scored by verifiable, state-based rewards. Experiments with 12 frontier MLLMs show they are still far from reliable assistants. Even the strongest model, Claude Fable 5.1, achieves at most 20% and 25% on long-horizon Guidance and Planning tasks, respectively. Overall, our work offers verifiable platform and rigorous evaluation set, laying solid"

Their responsibilities increasingly extend beyond short, narrowly scoped tasks to larger assignments that require them to maintain an overall objective, coordinate multiple stages, and carry the work through to completion. Furthermore, recursive self-improvement [2] explores how agents can improve their own models and runtime systems, bringing capability improvement itself into the range of work agents undertake. Humans are already delegating complex productivity tasks to agents across software engineering [3], office work [4, 5], web and computer use [68], enterprise operations [9], and scientific research [10]. Frontier agents such as Codex and Claude Code [11, 12] illustrate this shift by supporting multi-day, cross-session work with suitable orchestration and taking on parts of result assessment and feedback-driven revision previously handled by humans. Across these tasks, decisions often depend on evidence in related files, records, or applications and on states established by earlier actions. work-centric perspective traces these dependencies from the initial request through execution to verifiable outcome. From this perspective, we introduce the concept of work agent, defined as system that uses foundation model as its decision-making core, relies on runtime harness to organize environment observations and action execution, and carries out delegated tasks within stateful digital workspace. The foundation model provides understanding, reasoning, and decision-making capabilities; the harness manages context construction, tool invocation, memory, skills, execution control, and error recovery; and the workspace contains interconnected objects such as files, applications, records, tasks, and other actors. work agent aims to produce work outcomes that satisfy task requirements, with execution results verified through observable evidence. This perspective connects task objectives, model capabilities, system mechanisms, environment states, and verification, shifting the unit of analysis from isolated model outputs or tool calls to the complete process of accomplishing digital work. Work agents operate under different conditions depending on the tasks they undertake. To characterize these differences, we introduce two conceptually distinct yet complementary dimensions: ❶ work persistence and ❷ work openness. Work persistence concerns the conditions under which delegated responsibility can be considered complete, distinguishing work that concludes upon delivery from responsibilities that remain active ac"

Text:"October 27, 2026 Qwen-Image-PE: Learning Visual Design for Text-to-Image Prompt Enhancement Zekai Zhang, Yanran Zhang, Shengming Yin, Yan Shu, Zhening Liu, Bingxuan Zhao, Chenyang Zhu, Donghao Zhou, Jiahao Li, Jiahao Wang, Jie Zhang, Kaiyuan Gao, Kun Yan, Lihan Jiang, Ningyuan Tang, Qihang Ai, Rui Song, Shuo Cao, Xiao Xu, Xiaoyue Chen, Yuxiang Chen, Zhendong Wang, Zhouyang Li, Zihao Liu, Zikai Zhou, Bo Zheng, Huishuai Zhang, Dongyan Zhao, Chenfei WuLearning visual design is essential for prompt enhancement (PE) to turn underspecified user requests into coherent visual specifications for text-to-image (T2I) generation. Existing methods preserve user intent and improve overall visual appeal, with less explicit emphasis on fine-grained design dimensions. To address this gap, we introduce Qwen-Image-PE, which learns visual design through two-stage training. Supervised fine-tuning with Caption-Based Reverse Distillation (CRD) uses dense captions of welldesigned images as design supervision. Reinforcement learning with Groupwise Design Reward (GDR) jointly compares all candidate images for the same request to refine lighting, composition, layout, and detail. On Qwen-Image-Bench with Qwen-Image-2.1, Qwen-Image-PE-397B improves over original requests by 7.81 points, while QwenImage-PE-9B outperforms all evaluated PE baselines. Further evaluations show gains in visual design, aesthetics, image quality, and human preference. We find that PE scales along three axes: training data, model size, and information density, with sufficient information density as prerequisite for effective enhancement. Moreover, we demonstrate that Qwen-Image-PE generalizes to four additional T2I backbones, even surpassing native prompt enhancers on HunyuanImage-2.1 and FLUX 3 Image Klein. We release Qwen-Image-PE-9B at https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen-Image-2.1-PE-T2I. Figure 1: Qwen-Image-PE improves visual design. Within each pair, Qwen-Image-2.1 outputs from enhanced prompts (right) show more expressive lighting, more cohesive compositions, clearer layouts, and richer details than those from original requests (left). Corresponding author.Modern text-to-image (T2I) models can follow increasingly detailed visual specifications, offering control over lighting, composition, layout, and fine-grained detail (Wu et al., 2025b; Zhao et al., 2026; Black Forest Labs, 2026). However, user requests are often brief and leave many of these choices unspecified (Zhang et al., 2026). Prompt enhancement (PE) bridges this gap by expanding user requests into detailed prompts. The visual choices introduced during enhancement shape how the requested content is presented, making visual design an important capability for prompt enhancers. Existing prompt enhancement methods pursue semantic alignment by preserving and clarifying user intent (Wang et al., 2025; Boogu Team, 2026; ERNIE Team, Baidu, 2026; Black Forest Labs, 2026). Beyond semantic correctness, other approaches improve visual appeal through aesthetic or preference optimization (Hao et al., 2022; Cao et al., 2023; Tang et al., 2026; Chen et al., 2025). However, these approaches primarily optimize overall visual appeal, with less explicit emphasis on individual design dimensions. We therefore study how PE can explicitly learn and refine fine-grained design dimensions, including lighting, composition, layout, and detail. The challenge is how to translate fine-grained visual design into effective supervision targets and optimization feedback. Our key insight is that: (1) Well-designed images demonstrate how design choices work together. Dense captions make these choices explicit, providing concrete supervision for PE to learn visual design. (2) Jointly comparing all candidate images for the same request provides common basis for calibrating design scores. This groupwise comparison helps distinguish their relative quality, providing more informative feedback. Guided by these insights, we introduce Qwen-Image-PE, which learns visual design through two-stage training paradigm. First, supervised fine-tuning via Caption-Based Reverse Distillation (CRD) enables PE to imitate the design choices in well-designed images. We construct requestimage pairs, select images for design quality and request consistency, and densely caption their visual content and design choices to obtain enhanced-prompt targets. These captions, together with synthesized design rationales, provide supervision for learning visual design. Second, reinforcement learning with Groupwise Design Reward (GDR) refines the enhancer through visual comparison. For each request, we generate images from group of sampled enhanced prompts and arrange the images into grid. multimodal judge jointly scores the candidates under explicit design rubrics, conditioning each candidates scores on the full group. We combine these design scores with an alignment reward to optimize the enhancer while keeping the T2I backbone frozen. We evaluate Qwen-Image-PE on Qwen-Image-Bench (Li et al., 2026) for overall generation performance, complemented by assessments of visual design, aesthetics, image quality, and human preference. On Qwen-Image-Bench with common Qwen-Image-2.1 backbone, Qwen-Image-PE-397B improves the Overall score over original requests by 7.81 points, while Qwen-Image-PE-9B outperforms all evaluated prompt enhancement baselines in Overall. Complementary evaluations show gains in visual design, aesthetics, image quality, and human preference. We further study the scaling properties and generalization behavior of PE, with four main findings. (1) PE scales with training data, model size, and information density. (2) Sufficient information density is prerequisite for effective enhancement. (3) Both PE variants outperform original requests on four additional T2I backbones without access to their training captions. (4) On HunyuanImage-2.1 and FLUX 3 Image Klein, both variants outperform native prompt enhancers, suggesting that PE learns transferable visual design principles rather than generator-specific caption patterns. Our contributions are summarized as follows: Training paradigm of PE. We propose two-stage training paradigm for learning visual design. First, supervised fine-tuning via Caption-Based Reverse Distillation (CRD) enables PE to imitate the design choices in well-designed images. Second, reinforcement learning with Groupwise Design Reward (GDR) refines fine-grained design dimensions, including lighting, composition, la"

Yet self-modification alone does not guarantee trustworthy improvement. Reported gains may reflect flawed evaluations, previously acquired capabilities may deteriorate, and compliance with behavioral constraints may weaken. Compromised artifacts or sensitive information may also persist in successor systems. Changes to improvement mechanisms may invalidate earlier verification results and make human oversight less effective. Text:"Continual Learning Exam Benchmarking Long-Horizon RSI and Experiential Learning in LLM Agents Continual learning is the ability of LLM agents to improve over time by acquiring, refining, and applying knowledge from experience while retaining useful prior knowledge. It is essential for agents learning over long time horizons, yet current evaluations largely assess individual task performance rather than improvement through accumulated experience. We introduce Continual Learning Exam (CLE), large-scale benchmark developed by 50+ domain experts to measure experience-driven improvement in realistic professional workflows. CLE comprises 120 human-authored tasks, each organised as five-level ladder, yielding 600 levels spanning 89 professional roles across 12 occupational domains. Agents must pass each level to unlock the next, with later levels building on experience accumulated during earlier assignments. An evaluation agent assesses each submission against human-authored rubrics. We compare single-submission setting with one that provides diagnostic feedback after failure and allows one revision. Across 17 harnessmodel configurations using frontier LLMs, feedback and revision yield median completion gain of 5.3 percentage points. Yet the best-performing system clears only 47.7% of levels and completes all five levels in just 23.3% of tasks, highlighting the difficulty of sustained learning from experience for existing frontier LLMs. As such, the dataset can also be used to post-train LLMs to strengthen their continual learning capabilities. Text:"Continual Learning Exam Benchmarking Long-Horizon RSI and Experiential Learning in LLM AgentsContinual learning is the ability of LLM agents to improve over time by acquiring, refining, and applying knowledge from experience while retaining useful prior knowledge. It is essential for agents learning over long time horizons, yet current evaluations largely assess individual task performance rather than improvement through accumulated experience. We introduce Continual Learning Exam (CLE), large-scale benchmark developed by 50+ domain experts to measure experience-driven improvement in realistic professional workflows. CLE comprises 120 human-authored tasks, each organised as five-level ladder, yielding 600 levels spanning 89 professional roles across 12 occupational domains. Agents must pass each level to unlock the next, with later levels building on experience accumulated during earlier assignments. An evaluation agent assesses each submission against human-authored rubrics. We compare single-submission setting with one that provides diagnostic feedback after failure and allows one revision. Across 17 harnessmodel configurations using frontier LLMs, feedback and revision yield median completion gain of 5.3 percentage points. Yet the best-performing system clears only 47.7% of levels and completes all five levels in just 23.3% of tasks, highlighting the difficulty of sustained learning from experience for existing frontier LLMs. As such, the dataset can also be used to post-train LLMs to strengthen their continual learning capabilities. (cid:128) Homepage: Hugging Face: GitHub: # Correspondence: https://haoyu-zhao.github.io/CLE/ https://huggingface.co/Continual-Learning-Exam https://github.com/haoyu-zhao/Continual-Learning-Exam haoyu.zhao.25@ucl.ac.uk; jun.wang@cs.ucl.ac.uk Figure 1: CLE at Glance. (A) Illustration of continual learning: an agent accumulates experience en through interaction and uses the update operator Un to modify its model parameters θn, persistent harness state sn, or both. (B) Summary statistics of CLE. (C) Completion rates across 17 harnessmodel configurations, measured as the percentage of all 600 levels passed. Trial 1 allows one submission per level without diagnostic feedback; Trial 2 allows one revision and resubmission following diagnostic feedback on an initial failure. (D) The change in completion rate from Trial 1 to Trial 2, in percentage points (pp). See the appendix for the full author list and affiliations. 1. Introduction Continual Learning Exam For large language model (LLM) agents, continual learning unfolds over long time horizon, as agents acquire, refine, and apply knowledge across successive tasks and interactions while retaining useful prior knowledge [31, 49, 56]. This ability is essential for sustained deployment, enabling agents to adapt to changing environments and task demands while building on past experience and avoiding repeated mistakes. Typically, this accumulated experience can improve agents through updates to model parameters, using supervised fine-tuning or reinforcement learning [78, 79], or through adaptations to their harness while keeping model parameters fixed. Harness-based learning includes retaining information in long-term memory [12, 47, 81], extracting lessons through reflection [58, 89, 94], and developing reusable skills [64, 70, 73, 95]. For example, an agent may learn from failed tool call, refine its tool-use procedure, and reuse the improved procedure in subsequent tasks. Evaluating continual learning therefore requires tracking performance across sustained sequence of tasks to determine whether accumulated experience leads to lasting improvements while preserving previously acquired capabilities. However, most existing agent benchmarks assess individual task performance without directly measuring such improvement. This limitation arises from how they are constructed. General capability benchmarks such as GAIA evaluate whether agents solve individual problems [28, 40, 50]. Tasks are presented and scored independently; their scores therefore characterise task-solving performance, rather than how that performance improves through continued learning. Memory benchmarks such as LoCoMo test retrieval and reasoning over interaction histories through question answering [36, 75, 76], without evaluating how retained knowledge improves subsequent task execution. Benchmarks for Claw-based automation assistants, such as WildClawBench, evaluate agents in executable environments with persistent state, long tool-use trajectories, and cross-application workflows [17, 38, 83]. Persistent state supports continuity in workflow execution, but does not by itself establish that an agent becomes more capable through experience. Evaluating cumulative improvement requires sequence of task attempts that supports experience reuse under controlled feedback conditions. To address this evaluation gap, we introduce the Continual Learning Exam (CLE), large-scale benchmark for evaluating continual learning in realistic humanAI cowork settings. Created by 50+ industry experts, CLE comprises 120 human-authored and human-verified tasks spanning 89 professional roles across twelve professional domains, each organised as five-level ladder, for total of 600 levels. These tasks are supported by 23,981 real-world input files, 19 simulated applications, and 16,125 human-authored evaluation criteria. In the main evaluation setting, agents must pass each level to unlock the next. CLE also supports custom evaluation through blocks, defined as contiguous segments of task ladder that can begin at any level, with the required support files provided. Later levels introduce new assignments that build on knowledge and experience accumulated through earlier work. We design the task ladders around five forms of knowledge that agents can acquire, retain, and reuse across levels. These five forms reflect common scenarios encountered by LLM agents in real-world workflows. Tools and Skills capture reusable knowledge of how to use tools and carry out tasks, while Data Dependency connects later assignments to earlier information and artefacts. Strategy guides the choice of effective approaches and the avoidance of recurring pitfalls. User Context and Domain Knowledge help align task execution with users preferences and professional rules and conventions. CLE provides realistic environment for large-scale continual learning evaluation, supported by extensive worki"

Text:"Rethinking Video Storage for AI Training: Breaking Cross-Frame Dependencies for Efficient Random Access Zetian Song1, Haotian Zhang1, Qizhe Wang1, Shiyu Huang2, Jiaqi Zhang1, 1Peking University, 2Tsinghua University Equal contribution. , Siwei Ma1, Wen Gao1 Corresponding authors. , Qize Yu1, , Yue Chen1, Bohan Zhou1, Bowen Ping1, Yuqi Ye1, Haocheng Tang1, Large-scale AI training increasingly relies on massive video datasets with random temporal sampling patterns. However, existing video storage paradigms are optimized for sequential playback and employ sophisticated inter-frame reference structures to maximize compression efficiency. When applied to the random-sampling workloads of AI training, these paradigms face substantial decoding-efficiency bottlenecks. Workarounds such as independent JPEG frames and short groups of pictures (GOPs) reduce decoding work at the cost of larger storage and transfer volumes. In this paper, we identify two sources of decode amplification: sequential reconstruction of unrelated frames and reconstruction of the reference chains required by sampled targets. We address both sources by co-designing the decoding schedule and video reference structure. Dependency-aware sparse decoding reconstructs only requested frames and their required references, skipping unrelated reconstruction without transcoding the source videos. Optional offline Dependency-Decoupled Random Access (DDRA) encoding further reduces reference reconstruction by restricting each interior frame to two bounding intra-coded anchors. Together, sparse decoding and DDRA retain inter-frame prediction while bounding reconstruction to at most three frames per requested target, independent of the intra period. Experiments on large-scale robotics corpus show that, at comparable reconstruction quality, our method achieves 1.45 the loading throughput of LeRobot v3 with 58.7% less media payload. Date: October 21, 2026 Project Page: https://skywalker-yqz.github.io/codec4AI/ Code: https"

In 86% of failed weak-model runs, the violated requirement is already stated in the skill. This motivates skill adaptation: reformulating existing skills to match the executors capabilities and make their guidance easier to execute. To achieve this, we diagnose failures in tool execution, context management, instruction following, and verification, and distill these diagnoses into fixed meta-skill that guides adaptation during harness self-evolution. Text:"CausalAlign: Causal Alignment of Video Generation Models for Consistent Ego-Motion Kai Zhu1,, Yintianrun Zhang1,, Shuwei Jiang1, Ruolin Zuo1, Yixin Chen2, Duomin Wang3, Miao Liu4, Yin Li5, Daquan Zhou1, Yiwu Zhong1, 1Peking University, 2Beijing Institute for General Artificial Intelligence, 3NVIDIA, 4Tsinghua University, 5University of WisconsinMadison Equal contribution, Corresponding author Diffusion-based video generation models (VGMs) can produce visually plausible videos, yet their camera ego-motion often drifts over long horizons. Camera ego-motion determines how the viewpoint changes between frames and is essential for maintaining consistent geometry and trajectories. To address this problem, we propose CausalAlign, causal alignment framework with two components. First, we diagnose how precise ego motion is represented, used, and retained within VGMs using linear probes and causal interventions, revealing rise-and-fall pattern during denoising. Second, we strengthen ego-motion state in VGM by aligning the representations at the identified layers to 3D foundation model through delta-token space. This space encodes the changes between consecutive frames, allowing the alignment to focus on ego-motion dynamics rather than static scene content. With Wan2.2 fine-tuned on DL3DV and RealEstate10K, CausalAlign can largely improve motion accuracy, geometric consistency, and temporal smoothness, while preserving visual quality. For example, it improves geometric consistency by more than 20% relative to the base model. Moreover, with the fine-tuned VGM, CausalAlign improves the success rate of world action models (WAMs) by 10 points on mobile manipulation tasks of RoboCasa365 benchmark. Overall, CausalAlign improves ego-motion consistency in diffusion-based VGMs and yields noticeable gains in embodied models. Text:"Joint or Per-Option? Compact Encoders for Closed-Set Decisions from 17 Million to 1 Billion Parameters Tardelli Ronan Coelho Stekel Federal Institute of São Paulo (IFSP), São Paulo, Brazil stekel@ifsp.edu.br ORCID: 0000-0002-3135-3656 Abstract. Many language-model pipelines end in closed-set decision, such as selecting tool. Prompting large language model for it is slow and order-sensitive. We ask how small an encoder for such decisions can be and whether it should read the options jointly or separately. In three-seed study at 32 million parameters, per-option scoring beats joint scoring on held-out sample of 37-benchmark decision suite by 5.8 points of chance-corrected skill at matched optimizer steps and by 2.8 at approximately matched training compute; two external benchmarks agree, though not significantly on their unseen tasks. Crossing reader with initialisation shows that the advantage requires reranker initialisation, whose querycandidate pair input matches per-option reading; from the raw encoder the two readers are indistinguishable. Per-option scoring needs 5 to 13 times more encoder passes: at most twice the median latency, but more than four times the mean. Of six released joint encoders (17 million to 1 billion parameters), the 68and 150-million-parameter models exceed 435-million-parameter DeBERTa-v3 zero-shot classifier by 4.9 and 5.9 accuracy points on 209 overlap-audited System1-Arena tasks and are at least on par with it on the 145 tasks unseen in training. The smallest answers median request in 14 milliseconds on 16 processor threads, against 728 for the classifier. Reordering options changes 12 to 23% of the joint models answers (per-option scoring: none). Up to 68 million parameters, they lead their size range on public leaderboards private, mainly in-distribution tests. Index Terms. Text:"October 2026 Thinking-in-Bets at Test Time Reduces Sycophantic Revisions in LLM Agents Nghi D. Q. Bui Google Language model agents often abandon correct work when user challenges it, yet simply telling them to stand firm makes them ignore real mistakes. Once an agent reads complaint, it tends to discount its own earlier checks in order to agree with the user. We introduce Thinking-in-Bets, training-free method in which an agent writes short private record of its main claims, the checks supporting them, and its confidence before reading user feedback. When challenged, the agent consults this Claim Checklist to keep verified work intact while investigating uncertain or newly challenged parts. We evaluate five Gemini models (3.5 Flash-Lite, 3.5 Flash, 3.6 Flash, 3.8 Flash, and 4.0 Argon) across eight benchmarks: SWE-Together, SWE-Interact, and DeepSWE for software engineering, Agents Last Exam for professional reports, Humanitys Last Exam and SycEval for academic reasoning, Vals Finance for financial analysis, and Terminal-Bench for command-line tasks. Recording claims before criticism nearly eliminates false reversals in patch review and protects passing report drafts under mistaken pushback, whereas writing the same record after reading the complaint recovers very little of the gain. In multi-turn coding, pre-reply claim records substantially reduce unnecessary file edits after mistaken complaints, and gating the record so it is written only when the agent is uncertain lowers token overhead while fixing more real bugs than the baseline. Even in non-interactive tasks without user feedback, listing the key claims in draft and re-checking the weakest one before submission improves both answer accuracy and confidence estimates across all tested models. (cid:128) thinking-in-bets.github.io 1. How to make local generative assistant trustworthy when it is disconnected, and what trust costs on edge hardware, remain largely unmeasured. We propose connectivity-regime framework (C0C4) that treats disconnection as design dimension, taxonomy of inference-time trust mechanisms, and signed, versioned knowledge packs as the update channel for disconnected regimes. We then evaluate these ideas on Raspberry Pi 5 running fully local retrieval-augmented generation under network isolation, with three Qwen2.5 model sizes (1.5B, 3B, and 7B parameters), three context sizes, and 300 SQuAD 2.0 questions that are answerable, unanswerable, or outside the corpus. Abstract Trajectory2Gym improves tool-use training by constructing interactions with compositional coverage and decision grounding rather than collecting successful trajectories.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Successful tool-use trajectories are not necessarily useful supervision. Even successful interactions may underrepresent multi-step dependencies or contain actions that are unjustified by the information visible to the agent at decision time. We identify two coupled requirements for effective trajectory supervision: compositional coverage, so that important observation-action dependencies are represented, and decision grounding, so that each supervised action is supported by the learner-visible interaction history. We introduce Trajectory2Gym, which constructs executable interactions around under-supported compositional structures and derives supervision only from grounded decisions in their realized executions. Structural-support selection allocates the resulting experience, while parent-normalized supervised fine-tuning preserves the semantic interaction as the unit of training weight. This reframes trajectory generation as the deliberate construction of learnable interaction experience, rather than the collection of successful trajectories. Across five Qwen3 scales, Trajectory2Gym improves Tau1, Tau2, and BFCL at every tested scale, increasing the equal-weight three-benchmark mean by 16.9-29.8 percentage points. The gains are strongest in stateful and multi-turn interaction, where later decisions depend on earlier observations and feedback. Our results suggest that scaling tool-use agents depends not only on collecting more successful trajectories, but on constructing interactions whose dependencies are represented and whose decisions are learnable from the agent's visible history.
[27.10.2026 11:30] Response: {"desc": "Статья представляет Trajectory2Gym, метод обучения LLM-агентов использованию инструментов (tool-use), который строит исполняемые взаимодействия вокруг недостаточно представленных композиционных структур, а не просто собирает успешные траектории. Ключевые принципы: компositional coverage, чтобы важные зависимости «наблюдение-действие» были отражены, и decision grounding, чтобы каждое действие опиралось на историю взаимодействия, видимую агенту. Супервизия (SFT с нормализацией по родительскому взаимодействию) извлекается только из обоснованных решений, а выбор структур выделяет ресурсы обучения. Эксперименты на моделях Qwen3 разных масштабов показывают рост качества на Tau1, Tau2 и BFCL примерно на 17–30 п.п., особенно в stateful и многошаговых сценариях.", "emoji": "🧩", "title": "Учим агентов не просто успешным траекториям, а обучаемому опыту взаимодействия"}

We identify two coupled requirements for effective trajectory supervision: compositional coverage, so that important observation-action dependencies are represented, and decision grounding, so that each supervised action is supported by the learner-visible interaction history. Abstract StreamVibe introduces a benchmark, streaming vision-language model, and hierarchical memory harness to enable proactive, intent-aware interaction with implicit and evolving user goals.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Vision-language models are shifting from offline passive understanding toward online proactive interaction. However, prevailing methods largely rely on explicit requests while miss the implicit user intent (e.g., "where should I click next?" while a user navigate a website), meanwhile fail to update the state simultaneously as user requests might update continuously. These limitations make them less suited to intent-aware interaction in practical. To address these challenges, we introduce StreamVibe-Bench, a new benchmark serving as testbed for user intent interaction, with 4.1K evaluation samples and 85K training samples spanning explicit, implicit, and dynamic intents. Building on this benchmark, we develop StreamVibe-VL, a streaming proactive model that infers implicit user intents from continuous observations and dynamically tailors its interactions as these intents evolve. We further introduce StreamVibe-Harness, which maintains user intents as persistent states with a fleixble lifecycles via a hierarchical memory to track the latest user intent and environmental state. Our experiments reveal three key findings: (i) StreamVibe-VL preserves strong general video understanding while improving response timing and answer quality by 10.7% over other baselines; (ii) existing proactive models remain limited in understanding user intent and deciding when to respond, especially under implicit and dynamic intents; and (iii) StreamVibe-Harness further improves response consistency and adaptation over sustained interaction, resulting in a 12.3% gain in proactive interaction.

"Abstract StreamVibe introduces a benchmark, streaming vision-language model, and hierarchical memory harness to enable proactive, intent-aware interaction with implicit and evolving user goals.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Vision-language models are shifting from offline passive understanding toward online proactive interaction. However, prevailing methods largely rely on explicit requests while miss the implicit user intent (e.g., "where should I click next?" while a user navigate a website), meanwhile fail to update the state simultaneously as user requests might update continuously. These limitations make them less suited to intent-aware interaction in practical. To address these challenges, we introduce StreamVibe-Bench, a new benchmark serving as testbed for user intent interaction, with 4.1K evaluation samples and 85K training samples spanning explicit, implicit, and dynamic intents. Building on this benchmark, we develop StreamVibe-VL, a streaming proactive model that infers implicit user intents from continuous observations and dynamically tailors its interactions as these intents evolve. We further introduce StreamVibe-Harness, which maintains user intents as persistent states with a fleixble lifecycles via a hierarchical memory to track the latest user intent and environmental state. Our experiments reveal three key findings: (i) StreamVibe-VL preserves strong general video understanding while improving response timing and answer quality by 10.7% over other baselines; (ii) existing proactive models remain limited in understanding user intent and deciding when to respond, especially under implicit and dynamic intents; and (iii) StreamVibe-Harness further improves response consistency and adaptation over sustained interaction, resulting in a 12.3% gain in proactive interaction."

Abstract DynaFlow enables adaptive multi-agent collaboration by letting a main agent synthesize executable workflows that coordinate sub-agents through structured execution and verification.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Multi-agent systems often rely on predefined collaboration structures, limiting their ability to adapt to diverse task requirements. We introduce DynaFlow, a framework that integrates free-form executable workflow synthesis into the action space of the main agent. The agent decides when collaboration is needed and generates task-specific programs that coordinate sub-agents through sequential or parallel execution, structured information exchange, and verification. Execution results and traces inform subsequent actions, enabling the agent to adapt collaboration as reasoning progresses. We train only the main agent through supervised fine-tuning on distilled trajectories, followed by refinement on examples filtered for unsupported premises, biased verification, and information-flow defects. All sub-agents remain fixed, separating orchestration learning from executor capability improvement. Across six benchmarks spanning mathematical reasoning, scientific question answering, and multi-hop retrieval, DynaFlow achieves 88.08% overall accuracy in Forced mode, outperforming the strongest evaluated baseline by 10.90 percentage points with the same Qwen3.5-27B backbone. Workflow analysis finds more explicit use, verification, and refinement of upstream outputs after training, highlighting cross-stage information flow as a key dimension of multi-agent orchestration.

Обучается только основной агент через supervised fine-tuning на дистиллированных траекториях с последующим дообучением на отфильтрованных примерах, тогда как подагенты остаются фиксированными. Abstract TraceTrial-Bench evaluates agent harness reliability across seven components using mechanism-oriented tasks, trajectory tracing, and failure attribution to compare harness designs and diagnose failures.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small LLM agents are increasingly used to solve real-world problems, and their effectiveness depends on both the underlying models and the harnesses that support their operation. However, most existing benchmarks focus on model capabilities and overall agent performance, with limited attention to the evaluation of agent harnesses. To address this gap, we introduce TraceTrial-Bench, which organizes harness reliability into seven components covering context, execution, governance, lifecycle, observability, tools, and verification. We construct 95 expert-level, mechanism-oriented tasks with executable or state-based ground truth to support semantically equivalent comparisons while preserving the native behavior of each harness. We design a Trajectory Tracing (TT) module to capture agent execution traces and introduce a two-layer evaluation framework that separately assesses agent performance and harness reliability. In addition, we design a Multi-source Evidence Acquisition (MEA) module to provide evidence from the agent context, harness, and execution environment for LLM-based evaluation, and develop a Failure Attribution (FA) module that uses explicit mechanism chains to support attribution at the run level. Across 4 harnesses, 9 models, and 95 tasks, our evaluation of 3,420 runs yields macro-averaged harness scores ranging from 59.8% to 72.0%, while 31.1% of runs achieve task success despite failing at least one harness criterion. TraceTrial-Bench provides an auditable and reproducible foundation for comparing agent harnesses, diagnosing their failures, and studying how harness design interacts with model behavior.
[27.10.2026 11:31] Response: {"desc": "Статья представляет TraceTrial-Bench — бенчмарк для оценки надёжности agent harness, то есть обвязки, которая поддерживает работу LLM-агентов, а не только самих моделей. Надёжность разбита на семь компонентов: контекст, выполнение, governance, жизненный цикл, observability, инструменты и верификация, а тестирование проводится на 95 экспертных задачах с исполняемой или state-based разметкой. Для анализа используются модуль Trajectory Tracing, двухуровневая оценка, модуль Multi-source Evidence Acquisition для LLM-as-a-judge и Failure Attribution на основе механистических цепочек. Эксперименты на 3 420 запусках показывают разброс оценок harness от 59.8% до 72.0%, а 31.1% успешных по задаче запусков всё же нарушают хотя бы один критерий надёжности.", "emoji": "🔍", "title": "Успешный результат не всегда означает надёжного агента: оцениваем не только модель, но и её обвязку"}

TraceTrial-Bench provides an auditable and reproducible foundation for comparing agent harnesses, diagnosing their failures, and studying how harness design interacts with model behavior."

Paper text to classify:

"Abstract TraceTrial-Bench evaluates agent harness reliability across seven components using mechanism-oriented tasks, trajectory tracing, and failure attribution to compare harness designs and diagnose failures.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small LLM agents are increasingly used to solve real-world problems, and their effectiveness depends on both the underlying models and the harnesses that support their operation. However, most existing benchmarks focus on model capabilities and overall agent performance, with limited attention to the evaluation of agent harnesses. To address this gap, we introduce TraceTrial-Bench, which organizes harness reliability into seven components covering context, execution, governance, lifecycle, observability, tools, and verification. We construct 95 expert-level, mechanism-oriented tasks with executable or state-based ground truth to support semantically equivalent comparisons while preserving the native behavior of each harness. We design a Trajectory Tracing (TT) module to capture agent execution traces and introduce a two-layer evaluation framework that separately assesses agent performance and harness reliability. In addition, we design a Multi-source Evidence Acquisition (MEA) module to provide evidence from the agent context, harness, and execution environment for LLM-based evaluation, and develop a Failure Attribution (FA) module that uses explicit mechanism chains to support attribution at the run level. Across 4 harnesses, 9 models, and 95 tasks, our evaluation of 3,420 runs yields macro-averaged harness scores ranging from 59.8% to 72.0%, while 31.1% of runs achieve task success despite failing at least one harness criterion. TraceTrial-Bench provides an auditable and reproducible foundation for comparing agent harnesses, diagnosing their failures, and studying how harness design interacts with model behavior."

Abstract InteractiveWorld provides a verifiable simulation environment and benchmark to evaluate whether multimodal language models can reliably guide humans through physical tasks.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small People have long dreamed of an assistant that is always there to guide them and remind what they forget, from new dishes to lost keys. Powered by multimodal large language models (MLLMs), egocentric AI assistants on wearable devices are bringing this dream within reach: they see what the user sees and give instructions for user's physical work when necessary. However, testing such assistance is challenging and usually requires real people. The expensive and rare real interaction data leaves a key question unanswered: Can today's MLLMs truly help people in physical world? To answer this question, we propose InteractiveWorld, the first verifiable environment for closed-loop interaction between human and AI assistant. Unlike existing robot-centric simulators, InteractiveWorld lets the assistant guide a human agent who observes the scene, understands instructions, and acts independently. It thus realistically simulates how AI assistant collaborates with human: the assistant instructs, the human acts, and the environment executes actions and updates what they see next. Building on it, we introduce InteractiveBench, the first dynamic benchmark for egocentric AI assistants, with 120 multi-turn tasks across six categories. In each task, the assistant guides the human in real time, and every instruction shapes the outcome, which is scored by verifiable, state-based rewards. Experiments with 12 frontier MLLMs show they are still far from reliable assistants. Even the strongest model, Claude Fable 5.1, achieves at most 20% and 25% on long-horizon Guidance and Planning tasks, respectively. Overall, our work offers a verifiable platform and a rigorous evaluation set, laying a solid foundation for research on interactive MLLM assistants that truly help people in the physical world.

На её основе создан InteractiveBench — динамический бенчмарк из 120 многошаговых задач в шести категориях, оценка которых проводится по проверяемым state-based наградам. Эксперименты с 12 передовыми мультимодальными LLM показали, что даже лучшая модель достигает лишь 20% и 25% на долгосрочных задачах Guidance и Planning. Abstract The paper proposes a unified framework for work agents that integrates foundation models with runtime harnesses to execute complex digital tasks reliably, introducing dimensions of persistence and openness alongside capability families and evaluation metrics.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small As foundation models evolve from information generation to digital work execution, agents are increasingly capable of completing complex tasks across tools, applications, and workflows. However, existing research lacks a unified perspective on how agents reliably accomplish delegated work. To address this gap, we present a work-centric, system-level perspective that views work agents as systems combining foundation models and runtime harnesses to execute tasks in stateful workspaces and deliver verifiable outcomes. We introduce two complementary dimensions, work persistence and work openness, to characterize working conditions, and classify digital work into five forms according to task scope and organization. Building on this framework, we identify five core capability families required for reliable work execution and examine approaches to model optimization, harness optimization, and their joint optimization. We further analyze existing evaluation methods and metrics from the perspective of work outcomes. Finally, we discuss future research directions, including sustained work delegation, adaptation to open environments, and the interaction between work execution and capability improvement. This paper provides a unified framework for understanding, optimizing, and evaluating work agents, supporting the development of systems capable of delivering reliable, verifiable, and practically valuable work outcomes.

We introduce two complementary dimensions, work persistence and work openness, to characterize working conditions, and classify digital work into five forms according to task scope and organization. Building on this framework, we identify five core capability families required for reliable work execution and examine approaches to model optimization, harness optimization, and their joint optimization. We further analyze existing evaluation methods and metrics from the perspective of work outcomes. Finally, we discuss future research directions, including sustained work delegation, adaptation to open environments, and the interaction between work execution and capability improvement. This paper provides a unified framework for understanding, optimizing, and evaluating work agents, supporting the development of systems capable of delivering reliable, verifiable, and practically valuable work outcomes."

Abstract CLACT and WorldCast enable consistent character video generation under large-angle camera motions by combining 3D point-cloud guidance with geometry-aligned memory attention.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Precise camera control is essential for cinematic character video generation, yet existing methods struggle to follow large-angle trajectories with combined rotation, translation, and radius variation from arbitrary initial azimuths. Such viewpoint changes also reveal character regions unseen in the initial frame, causing geometric distortions, appearance degradation, and identity drift. To systematically study these coupled challenges, we introduce CLACT, a large-scale Character-centric dataset and benchmark covering diverse Large-Angle Camera Trajectories, with accurate camera geometry and multi-view character observations for training and evaluation. We further propose WorldCast, a framework for world-consistent character video generation under diverse large-angle camera motions. Specifically, we back-project the initial frame into a 3D point cloud using estimated depth, align the point-cloud scale with the target camera trajectory, and render it along the trajectory to obtain explicit geometric guidance and coverage masks. To complement the incomplete character information, we introduce Geometry-Aligned Memory Attention (GAMA). Using relative camera poses estimated jointly from the initial frame and multi-view character anchors, we express anchor and target-view geometry in a common coordinate frame. GAMA uses this geometric alignment to guide the retrieval and injection of view-relevant character features during generation. Evaluations across diverse large-angle camera trajectories demonstrate accurate trajectory following while preserving character geometry, appearance, and identity.

Метод WorldCast строит 3D point cloud из первого кадра по оценке глубины, масштабирует его под целевую траекторию камеры и рендерит для получения геометрических подсказок и масок покрытия. Для восполнения невидимых в первом кадре областей персонажа используется механизм GAMA (Geometry-Aligned Memory Attention), который через относительные позы камеры выравнивает геометрию и извлекает релевантные признаки. Abstract AMBIT is a unified framework that generates and edits first-order ambisonics audio by coupling autoregressive scene planning with rectified-flow rendering, using a specialized continuous VAE and on-policy self-distillation to preserve spatial structure and acoustic fidelity.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Spatial audio authoring coordinates source decisions with soundfield synthesis. For editing, plans specify what to change and retain, while reference audio supplies further acoustic detail. We present AMBIT, a unified framework for native First-Order Ambisonics (FOA) generation and editing with three connected contributions. First, a shared Transfusion backbone couples autoregressive planning with rectified-flow rendering through executable ScenePlans that specify source content, activity, and 3D trajectories. For editing, the plan describes the full target scene, including retained sources; contrastive features ground the requested change, while full reference latents supply residual acoustic detail for preservation. Second, an FOA-aware continuous VAE combines shared-decoder omnidirectional recovery, asymmetric grouped KL, and spatial covariance matching to preserve content and inter-channel structure. Third, joint Transfusion on-policy self-distillation (OPSD) refines planning on student-generated prefixes and rendering at sampling states reached under student plans, with target-conditioned teachers supervising both modes of the shared student. Evaluations across speech, music, and sound show improved generation content and spatial quality, acoustic fidelity during instruction-guided editing, and unedited-source preservation over the compared baselines. Project page: https://wjm-george.github.io/AMBIT/
[27.10.2026 11:31] Response: {"desc": "Статья представляет AMBIT — единую модель для генерации и редактирования пространственного аудио в формате First-Order Ambisonics (FOA). Авторское решение объединяет авторегрессионное планирование сцены (ScenePlan с источниками, активностью и 3D-траекториями) и рендеринг через rectified flow на общем бэкбоне Transfusion. Для точного сохранения пространственной структуры используется специализированный непрерывный VAE с асимметричным grouped KL и согласованием пространственной ковариации. Обучение дополнительно усиливается joint on-policy self-distillation (OPSD), где целевые учителя направляют student-модель на собственных траекториях, и модель показывает улучшение качества на речи, музыке и звуках.", "emoji": "🎧", "title": "Объёмный звук по инструкции: планируй сцену и рендерь её целиком"}

Abstract FORK jointly optimizes recall and precision via rubric-driven necessity credits and factual grading to improve response quality and reduce verbosity in reinforcement learning.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Rubric-based rewards enable effective reinforcement learning on non-verifiable tasks. However, because these rewards are typically defined as a weighted measure of rubric coverage, models are trained primarily to maximize a recall objective. As a result, RL-trained models produce responses that are more complete but also more verbose and contain more unnecessary or factually incorrect content, leading them to be less preferred than responses from the base model. To address this issue, we present the FORK reward, which jointly optimizes recall and a novel precision objective, which requires each unit of a model response to be both necessary and factually correct, as measured by rubric-driven necessity credits and a factual-correctness grader, respectively. Our experimental results show that FORK outperforms existing rubric-based RL methods and achieves Pareto-optimal performance that balances rubric coverage and overall response quality. Compared to the original recall-based reward, FORK substantially improves precision and reduces response length while achieving similar rubric coverage, which leads to significantly higher win rate compared to the base policy (e.g., from 10.6% to 68.0% on RubricHub-Medical and from 22.1% to 74.1% on HealthBench).

Классические rubric-based награды поощряют только полноту (recall), из-за чего LLM становятся многословными и допускают больше лишних и фактических ошибок. FORK совместно оптимизирует recall и новую метрику precision, которая требует, чтобы каждая единица ответа была необходимой (по rubric-driven necessity credits) и фактически корректной (по factual-correctness grader). Эксперименты показывают, что FORK достигает Парето-оптимального баланса между покрытием rubric и общим качеством, заметно улучшая precision, сокращая длину ответов и повышая win rate по сравнению с базовой моделью на RubricHub-Medical и HealthBench.", "emoji": "⚖️", "title": "Полнота без воды: точность и полнота вместе в обучении LLM"}

Abstract Principia is an LLM-based framework that builds and iteratively refines scientific principles during inference to accelerate discovery across molecular and biological tasks.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Scientific discovery requires identifying the scientific principles underlying observed phenomena to guide further investigation. As these empirical principles are often unknown in complex discovery processes, they are constructed via a hypotheses-to-principle pipeline to obtain recurring causalities from progressively accumulated observations during test-time discovery. However, constructing this knowledge requires substantial domain expertise, time, and effort, making this translation stage a bottleneck for scientific discovery. To address this limitation, we propose Principia, an LLM-based framework that constructs and continually refines scientific principles at test time. Drawing on how human researchers develop principles, Principia forms comparative causal hypotheses from related inputs and their observed outcomes, then synthesizes recurring relationships across these hypotheses into inductive principles. By applying the constructed hypotheses and inductive principles to the task, it obtains new observations, revises the hypotheses used based on these new observations, and reconstructs principles from the updated hypotheses, allowing scientific knowledge to continuously evolve throughout test-time discovery. Across benchmarks spanning molecular optimization, protein engineering, gene perturbation, and molecular property prediction, Principia outperforms baselines relying on internal knowledge, external sources, or experience summaries, while generating principles that are plausible and consistent with the scientific literature, demonstrating its effectiveness in constructing and refining task-specific hypotheses and principles at test time for scientific discovery. Our code is available at https://github.com/DongkiKim95/Principia.

Система формулирует сравнительные причинно-следственные гипотезы по связанным входам и их результатам, а затем обобщает повторяющиеся зависимости в индуктивные принципы. Эти принципы применяются к задаче, новые наблюдения используются для пересмотра гипотез и восстановления принципов, что позволяет знаниям непрерывно эволюционировать. Abstract World-in-Context unifies view, action, and camera controls via explicit tokens and interaction-aware positional encoding to improve interactive video generation and navigation.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Interactive video world models require coordinated control over viewing perspectives, character actions, and camera motion. Existing methods typically leave view modes implicit in text captions, conflate action with camera motion, and inject controls through fragmented architectural pathways that capture interdependencies only indirectly. In this work, we present World-in-Context, an interactive world modeling framework powered by In-Context Conditioning (ICC). Rather than relying on specialized conditioning pathways, World-in-Context unifies view, action, and camera controls into explicit tokens contextualized directly within a shared self-attention space. Our formulation reveals that (i) explicit view tokens disentangle conflicting dynamics and unlock emergent view switching from single-view data, while (ii) action and camera inputs play complementary roles in governing local responsiveness and global stability. To preserve temporal continuity across execution intervals, we further introduce Interaction-Transport RoPE (IT-RoPE), parameterizing temporal attention with accumulated control transitions. On WBench, World-in-Context-5B achieves navigation performance on par with 22B EchoWM (81.5 vs. 81.6) and attains a 93.8% perspective-switching success rate, substantially outperforming existing 5B baselines (18.8%). Controlled analyses further demonstrate compounding advantages of World-in-Context under richer conditioning mixtures, favorable data efficiency, and natural applicability to causal rollouts.
[27.10.2026 11:32] Response: {"desc": "Авторы представляют World-in-Context — фреймворк для интерактивных world models, который объединяет управление точкой зрения, действиями персонажа и движением камеры через явные токены в общем пространстве self-attention, используя In-Context Conditioning (ICC). Явные view-токены разделяют конфликтующие динамики и позволяют моделировать переключение ракурса даже на данных с одним ракурсом, а действия и камера дополняют друг друга, отвечая за локальную отзывчивость и глобальную стабильность. Для сохранения временной непрерывности предложен IT-RoPE, который параметризует temporal attention накопленными переходами управления. Модель World-in-Context-5B на WBench сопоставима по навигации с 22B EchoWM и достигает 93.8% успешности переключения перспективы против 18.8% у 5B-базовых моделей.", "emoji": "🎬", "title": "Единый контекст управления делает интерактивные видеомиры управляемыми и согласованными"}

"Abstract World-in-Context unifies view, action, and camera controls via explicit tokens and interaction-aware positional encoding to improve interactive video generation and navigation.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Interactive video world models require coordinated control over viewing perspectives, character actions, and camera motion. Existing methods typically leave view modes implicit in text captions, conflate action with camera motion, and inject controls through fragmented architectural pathways that capture interdependencies only indirectly. In this work, we present World-in-Context, an interactive world modeling framework powered by In-Context Conditioning (ICC). Rather than relying on specialized conditioning pathways, World-in-Context unifies view, action, and camera controls into explicit tokens contextualized directly within a shared self-attention space. Our formulation reveals that (i) explicit view tokens disentangle conflicting dynamics and unlock emergent view switching from single-view data, while (ii) action and camera inputs play complementary roles in governing local responsiveness and global stability. To preserve temporal continuity across execution intervals, we further introduce Interaction-Transport RoPE (IT-RoPE), parameterizing temporal attention with accumulated control transitions. On WBench, World-in-Context-5B achieves navigation performance on par with 22B EchoWM (81.5 vs. 81.6) and attains a 93.8% perspective-switching success rate, substantially outperforming existing 5B baselines (18.8%). Controlled analyses further demonstrate compounding advantages of World-in-Context under richer conditioning mixtures, favorable data efficiency, and natural applicability to causal rollouts."

Abstract Qwen-Image-PE improves text-to-image prompts via two-stage training with caption-based reverse distillation and groupwise design rewards, scaling across data, model size, and information density.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Learning visual design is essential for prompt enhancement (PE) to turn underspecified user requests into coherent visual specifications for text-to-image (T2I) generation. Existing methods preserve user intent and improve overall visual appeal, with less explicit emphasis on fine-grained design dimensions. To address this gap, we introduce Qwen-Image-PE, which learns visual design through two-stage training. Supervised fine-tuning with Caption-Based Reverse Distillation (CRD) uses dense captions of well-designed images as design supervision. Reinforcement learning with Groupwise Design Reward (GDR) jointly compares all candidate images for the same request to refine lighting, composition, layout, and detail. On Qwen-Image-Bench with Qwen-Image-2.1, Qwen-Image-PE-397B improves over original requests by 7.81 points, while Qwen-Image-PE-9B outperforms all evaluated PE baselines. Further evaluations show gains in visual design, aesthetics, image quality, and human preference. We find that PE scales along three axes: training data, model size, and information density, with sufficient information density as a prerequisite for effective enhancement. Moreover, we demonstrate that Qwen-Image-PE generalizes to four additional T2I backbones, even surpassing native prompt enhancers on HunyuanImage-2.1 and FLUX 3 Image Klein. We release Qwen-Image-PE-9B at https://huggingface.co/Qwen/Qwen-Image-2.1-PE-T2I.
[27.10.2026 11:32] Response: {"desc": "Статья представляет Qwen-Image-PE, модель для улучшения промптов (prompt enhancement) для генерации изображений по тексту, которая учится визуальному дизайну в два этапа. На первом этапе используется supervised fine-tuning с Caption-Based Reverse Distillation, где в качестве супервизии выступают плотные описания хорошо оформленных изображений. На втором этапе применяется reinforcement learning с Groupwise Design Reward, который сравнивает все кандидаты для одного запроса и улучшает освещение, композицию, верстку и детали. Авторы показывают, что качество растет с увеличением объема данных, размера модели и плотности информации, а метод хорошо переносится на другие T2I-модели.", "emoji": "🎨", "title": "Обучаем промпты думать как дизайнер"}

Abstract This survey defines recursive self-improvement and organizes trustworthy advancement requirements into improvement validity, constraint preservation, and oversight across recursive update stages.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small AI systems built around large models increasingly use experience and feedback to make persistent changes to models, prompts, memories, tools, and workflows. Recursive self-improvement (RSI) extends this process to inductive biases and to the mechanisms that govern future updates, which can themselves be revised and reused across improvement cycles. Yet self-modification alone does not guarantee trustworthy improvement. Reported gains may reflect flawed evaluations, previously acquired capabilities may deteriorate, and compliance with behavioral constraints may weaken. Compromised artifacts or sensitive information may also persist in successor systems. Changes to improvement mechanisms may invalidate earlier verification results and make human oversight less effective. This survey presents an operational definition and process framework for RSI and organizes the requirements for trustworthy RSI into four interconnected themes: genuine and sustained improvement; behavioral constraints, alignment, and fairness; system security and privacy; and transparency and controllability. We examine these themes from three perspectives: Improvement Validity, Constraint Preservation, and Effective Oversight. This analysis covers improvement diagnosis, proposal generation, proposal evaluation, and update implementation (S1--S4), including the recursive application of these stages to improvement mechanisms (RI). We review risks, safeguards, and evaluation evidence for individual updates, effects inherited by successor systems, and revisions to improvement mechanisms, while distinguishing direct RSI findings from evidence in related systems. Finally, we examine evaluation settings, identify requirements for independent and longitudinal assessment, and discuss applications and open research problems.
[27.10.2026 11:32] Response: {"desc": "В статье дается определение рекурсивного самосовершенствования (RSI) — процесса, в котором AI-система меняет не только модели, промпты и инструменты, но и собственные механизмы будущих обновлений. Авторы выделяют четыре взаимосвязанных требования к надежному RSI: реальное и устойчивое улучшение, соблюдение поведенческих ограничений, безопасность и приватность, а также прозрачность и управляемость. Анализ строится через три перспективы: валидность улучшений, сохранение ограничений и эффективный надзор над стадиями диагностики, генерации, оценки и внедрения обновлений. Работа также рассматривает риски, передающиеся последующим системам, и необходимость независимой долгосрочной оценки.", "emoji": "🔄", "title": "Самосовершенствование AI без потери контроля и доверия"}

Yet self-modification alone does not guarantee trustworthy improvement. Reported gains may reflect flawed evaluations, previously acquired capabilities may deteriorate, and compliance with behavioral constraints may weaken. Compromised artifacts or sensitive information may also persist in successor systems. Changes to improvement mechanisms may invalidate earlier verification results and make human oversight less effective. This survey presents an operational definition and process framework for RSI and organizes the requirements for trustworthy RSI into four interconnected themes: genuine and sustained improvement; behavioral constraints, alignment, and fairness; system security and privacy; and transparency and controllability. We examine these themes from three perspectives: Improvement Validity, Constraint Preservation, and Effective Oversight. This analysis covers improvement diagnosis, proposal generation, proposal evaluation, and update implementation (S1--S4), including the recursive application of these stages to improvement mechanisms (RI). We review risks, safeguards, and evaluation evidence for individual updates, effects inherited by successor systems, and revisions to improvement mechanisms, while distinguishing direct RSI findings from evidence in related systems. Finally, we examine evaluation settings, identify requirements for independent and longitudinal assessment, and discuss applications and open research problems."

Abstract Δτ-Bench evaluates agent adaptability to evolving tool ecosystems, and Steward improves sustained success through incremental skill maintenance and dynamic provisioning.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small In this paper, we introduce Δτ-Bench, an executable agent benchmark that models the structural evolution of large tool ecosystems. In Δτ-Bench, independently evolving services continually reshape how tasks can be accomplished: familiar tools may disappear or be replaced, while more efficient alternatives emerge amid overlapping functionality and noisy documentation. Against this moving capability frontier, we evaluate whether agents can proactively discover efficient tool-use paths and promptly revise their accumulated procedural knowledge, bringing together demands that existing benchmarks fail to examine. Δτ-Bench comprises 1,588 validated multi-turn tasks across 42 domains and six successive ecosystem states generated through capability-preserving transformations. Our evaluation shows that even the strongest evaluated model or harness achieves only 42.3% task success, while initial gains from existing skill-learning methods can not consistently translate into better adaptation as the ecosystem evolves. These findings motivate Steward, an ecosystem manager that jointly manages tools and procedural knowledge through incremental skill maintenance and dynamic provisioning during task execution. Experiments show that Steward sustains higher task success across ecosystem changes with lower skill-maintenance overhead, while improving both recovery from disrupted workflows and adoption of more efficient alternatives.

Abstract A new benchmark evaluates whether LLM agents improve through repeated professional tasks with feedback, revealing limited sustained learning in current frontier models.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Continual learning is the ability of LLM agents to improve over time by acquiring, refining, and applying knowledge from experience while retaining useful prior knowledge. It is essential for agents learning over long time horizons, yet current evaluations largely assess individual task performance rather than improvement through accumulated experience. We introduce Continual Learning Exam (CLE), a large-scale benchmark developed by 50+ domain experts to measure experience-driven improvement in realistic professional workflows. CLE comprises 120 human-authored tasks, each organised as a five-level ladder, yielding 600 levels spanning 89 professional roles across 12 occupational domains. Agents must pass each level to unlock the next, with later levels building on experience accumulated during earlier assignments. An evaluation agent assesses each submission against human-authored rubrics. We compare a single-submission setting with one that provides diagnostic feedback after failure and allows one revision. Across 17 harness-model configurations using frontier LLMs, feedback and revision yield a median completion gain of 5.3 percentage points. Yet the best-performing system clears only 47.7% of levels and completes all five levels in just 23.3% of tasks, highlighting the difficulty of sustained learning from experience for existing frontier LLMs. As such, the dataset can also be used to post-train LLMs to strengthen their continual learning capabilities.

Набор содержит 120 задач, каждая из которых разбита на пять уровней, которые открываются последовательно, а поздние уровни опираются на опыт предыдущих. Оценку выполняет отдельный LLM-агент по рубрикам, написанным экспертами, и сравниваются режимы без обратной связи и с диагностикой и одной ревизией. Результаты показывают, что обратная связь даёт небольшой прирост, но лучшая система проходит лишь 47,7% уровней, что указывает на слабую способность современных frontier-моделей к устойчивому обучению на опыте.", "emoji": "🎓", "title": "Современные LLM-агенты пока плохо учатся на собственном опыте"}

Abstract AV-FABLE introduces a long-form audio-visual benchmark for sparse manipulations, showing that frozen perception features of audio-visual large language models contain forensic evidence best recovered via separate temporal decoders rather than language outputs.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Localized AI manipulations in long-form videos often affect brief events within otherwise authentic content, making video-level classification insufficient for forensic analysis. Existing audio-visual deepfake benchmarks largely focus on short talking-head clips, while long-form temporal forensics remains predominantly visual. We introduce AV-FABLE, a dual-timeline benchmark for sparse manipulations in open-scene long-form audio-visual videos. AV-FABLE contains 16,650 pristine--manipulated twin pairs (998 hours) created with 27 editing configurations, with separate audio and visual timelines that enable modality-specific temporal localization and expose errors hidden by joint-timeline evaluation. On AV-FABLE, zero-shot prompting of audio-visual large language models (AV-LLMs) yields near-chance detection and poor temporal localization. In contrast, supervised readouts of their frozen perception features recover strong forensic signals, suggesting manipulation evidence is encoded in these representations but not effectively exposed through language outputs. Motivated by this observation, we propose NFSR(Native Forensics via Separated Readouts), which reads frozen AV-LLM features with separate audio and visual temporal decoders. NFSR achieves the best audio-visual localization performance in-domain and across all three out-of-distribution settings, including transfer to held-out videos from an external long-video forensic benchmark. AV-FABLE, code, and model weights will be publicly released.

Показано, что zero-shot промптинг AV-LLM дает почти случайное качество детекции, однако supervised-классификаторы поверх замороженных признаков (frozen features) извлекают сильные forensic-сигналы. На основе этого предложен метод NFSR, который использует раздельные временные декодеры для аудио и визуальной части и достигает лучшей точности локализации как in-domain, так и на out-of-distribution данных. Abstract S3AC introduces a stabilized actor-critic training method with difficulty-aware prompt sampling to improve long reasoning in large language models without relying on grouped rollouts.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Critic-free methods are widely used in reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) for long chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning, avoiding difficult and costly critic training. Common alternatives lack token-level credit assignment and typically rely on grouped rollouts to estimate prompt-specific baselines. A critic can address both limitations, but long-CoT critic training is hindered by insufficient coverage of trajectories with diverse lengths and early gradients dominated by the newly initialized value head. We introduce S3AC (Stabilized, Selective, and Scalable Actor-Critic) for LLM reasoning, built around a practical critic-training recipe. The trained critic provides token-specific advantages and prompt-specific baselines. Using the prompt-level baseline to estimate difficulty, we propose Difficulty-Aware Prompt Sampling (DAPS). DAPS combines this estimate with outcome history to select prompts before generation, reducing rollout waste without offline difficulty initialization. S3AC improves group-rollout performance across dense and mixture-of-experts models from 1.5B to 397B parameters, outperforming a modern GRPO variant (GRPO-X). At 35B, it sustains single-rollout training while tested critic-free baselines deteriorate after initial gains.

Авторы предлагают практичный рецепт обучения критика, который даёт токен-уровневые advantage и промпт-специфичные baseline без необходимости групповых rollout. Для отбора сложности промптов вводится Difficulty-Aware Prompt Sampling (DAPS), который сокращает расточительную генерацию ещё до rollout. Abstract EmbodiedLab evaluates embodied agents that must physically interact with simulated laboratory apparatus to discover scientific principles through self-directed experimentation.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Embodied agents are expected to accomplish real-world goals by deciding for themselves how and when to interact with their surroundings. For many such goals, changing the physical state of the world is the means rather than the end: the agent changes the state of a system through interaction, observes the feedback, and explores until it either achieves the goal itself (e.g., discovering physical laws) or learns what the goal requires; it acts to know. Manipulation benchmarks stop at acting to change, with the target state given; scientific discovery benchmarks evaluate knowing but replace physical experimentation with symbolic actions or numerical queries. We introduce EmbodiedLab, a benchmark in which a robot must act to know. Quantitative science experiments make this setting controllable: the parameters of the system are resampled for every instance, so the result cannot be known in advance, yet it can be scored exactly. Across 30 experiments in five disciplines on 14 apparatus platforms, the agent receives an objective and the apparatus, chooses its own procedure, and operates a physics-based simulated laboratory, reading the instruments through its cameras. Evaluation checks what the agent came to know, grounded in readings recorded during the episode, as well as the physical state the task requires; every experiment is verified to be solvable. We evaluate six frontier models in their developers' coding agents with native observation and control, with added perception and motion planning, and with manipulation skills distilled from the strongest agents. With native control, even the strongest agent succeeds less than half the time; for every agent, most failures arise in interacting with the apparatus, and manipulation skills help weaker agents more than perception and planning.
